Security · OpenAI — Lead storyNov. 7

OpenAI’s GPT-6 Cyber Found 81 Hidden Firefox Flaws — And 948 Ways Into Linux

OpenAI’s newest model does not just spot security holes — it climbs through them. GPT-6 Cyber found 81 flaws nobody knew Firefox had, helped build working exploits for 948 Linux kernel bugs, and still stayed inside its guardrails more than 99% of the time.

OpenAI has launched GPT-6 Cyber, a model built for one job: finding vulnerabilities in software and helping fix them before someone hostile finds them first. This is not a general chatbot with security skills bolted on. It is a purpose-built tool for defenders — scan the code, spot the weakness, work out whether it can actually be exploited, and point at the fix.

Start with Firefox. In testing, GPT-6 Cyber uncovered 81 previously unknown high-severity flaws in the browser. The words that matter are “previously unknown.” These were not bugs picked off a public list. They were holes in one of the most scrutinised pieces of software on Earth — reviewed for years by Mozilla’s engineers and outside researchers — that human reviewers and earlier tools had walked straight past. Every one of them was a door nobody knew existed, in a browser millions of people trust precisely because it is open and heavily audited.

The Linux results go further. GPT-6 Cyber helped develop exploits for 948 vulnerabilities in the Linux kernel — the core of the operating system that runs most of the world’s servers and cloud infrastructure. Finding a flaw is step one; building a working exploit is what proves the flaw is genuinely dangerous and not a theoretical worry. On ExploitGym, the benchmark that tests exactly that skill, the model scored 48.5%, against 32.1% for its predecessor, GPT-5.6 Cyber. In plain English, its ability to turn a known weakness into a working break-in improved by half in a single generation.

Power, though, is only half the story. The other half is whether the tool behaves. OpenAI says GPT-6 Cyber went out of bounds less than 1% of the time in testing — it stuck to its assignment instead of wandering off into systems and actions it was never asked to touch. For a machine designed to break into software on purpose, that restraint is not a footnote. It is the product.

OpenAI is handling its own creation accordingly. The company has classified GPT-6 Cyber as Critical under its Preparedness Framework — its most serious cybersecurity rating — and access is limited to approved customers of Daybreak Red, its programme for vetted professional defenders. There is no open sign-up. And OpenAI has turned the hunter on itself, partnering with Hacktron to hunt vulnerabilities in OpenAI’s own systems, on the logic that the company should eat its own cooking before anyone else is served.

Why it matters: the argument about whether AI can hack is over. The live questions now are who gets a model this good at it, and whether the defence gets it first. OpenAI’s answer is a Critical rating, a hand-picked customer list, and a model that — on its maker’s numbers — colours inside the lines more than 99% of the time.

Models · Google — Lead storyNov. 7

Google’s Gemini Omni 1.2 Flash Makes 15-Second AI Video Cheaper — $0.07 a Second

Fifteen seconds of finished, editable AI video now costs about a dollar — and you fix it by talking to it. Google’s Gemini Omni 1.2 Flash just went on general sale: longer clips, better consistency, and a price cut built to hurt.

Google has made Gemini Omni 1.2 Flash generally available in the Gemini API and its business tools. Generally available is the phrase that matters: no waitlist, no preview label, no “selected partners.” Any developer or company can build on it today, which turns a demo the industry admired into a product rivals now have to price against.

The clips get longer. Omni 1.2 Flash generates up to 15 seconds of video, up from 10 — a 50% jump that sounds small until you remember that 15 seconds is the native length of the short-form video the internet actually runs on: the ad slot, the product clip, the social post. The bigger change is how you edit. Instead of re-rolling a fresh prompt and hoping, you edit conversationally — generate a clip, then give follow-up prompts that revise it, keeping what worked and changing what did not. Direction, not dice.

Google says the consistency problems that made AI video feel drunk are much improved: characters stay the same character from first frame to last, reference images hold their look when you steer a scene with an example picture, and lip sync — mouths matching the words they are meant to be saying — is tighter. Those three fixes are unglamorous, and they are exactly the difference between a fun toy and footage a business can put its name on.

Then the price. At 720p, Google has cut the cost 30%, to $0.07 per second of video. Do the arithmetic: a full 15-second clip costs about a dollar in output. Reports say the model also topped major AI video leaderboards on release — leaderboard claims deserve the usual pinch of salt, but a model does not have to be the undisputed champion for a 30% price cut at this quality to move the market. It just has to be good enough, and cheaper than the crew, the camera and the day rate it replaces.

Why it matters: video has always been the most expensive content a business can make, which is why most small businesses never made any. At seven cents a second, with editing done by conversation, the bottleneck stops being budget and starts being imagination — and every competitor still paying film-crew prices is now competing against a dollar.

Models · Open WeightsNov. 7

Mira Murati’s Thinking Machines Drops Inkling 2 Small: A 594-Billion-Parameter Open Challenger

The “Small” is doing heavy irony. Thinking Machines — the lab founded by former OpenAI chief technology officer Mira Murati — has launched Inkling 2 Small, a multimodal mixture-of-experts model carrying 594 billion parameters in total. By raw size, that puts it in the same weight class as the largest models ever announced. The trick is in how little of it wakes up at once.

A mixture-of-experts model works like a newsroom of specialists rather than one giant brain: each question is routed to the experts relevant to it, and the rest stay asleep. Inkling 2 Small activates only 17 billion parameters per token — under 3% of the whole. That is the bargain the architecture promises: frontier-scale knowledge on the shelf, small-model running costs at the till. It is also multimodal, built to see as well as read, and it is aimed squarely at the work agents actually do — coding, tool use, visual reasoning and research automation — rather than at winning a chat contest.

Access comes in two stages, and the order is the story. Tinker access — using the model through Thinking Machines’ own platform — is live now. The downloadable weights, the part that makes an “open” model genuinely open, are being held back until safety checks are complete. Independent scrutiny first, keys for everyone second. Open-weights releases have often raced to publish and answered questions later; Murati’s lab is deliberately running the sequence the other way round.

The positioning is just as deliberate. Inkling 2 Small is being billed as a leading Western open-weight model — and that framing points at the map as it actually stands. Much of the strongest open-weights progress of the past two years has come from Chinese labs, while the big American labs have kept their best models behind APIs and subscriptions. A well-funded US lab, run by one of the best-known builders in the field, planting a flag in open weights changes who the open ecosystem has to take seriously.

Why it matters: open weights are where AI turns into infrastructure — models that companies, researchers and governments can download, own and build on without renting intelligence by the token. If Inkling’s weights land as promised and the performance holds up outside the lab, the West gets a flagship it can actually hold in its hands, from a founder who helped build the closed era she is now challenging.

Safety · EvaluationNov. 7

Claude Is Now Building Claude — Anthropic’s Progress Is Speeding Up 2x, But You Can’t See All the Proof

The flywheel is no longer a metaphor. METR, the independent assessor that labs invite in to measure what their models can really do, reported on November 5 that Anthropic’s AI capability progress is now running at roughly twice its previous pace. The study window was August 12 to October 29 — under three months — and the finding is not that Anthropic got better. It is that the rate at which Anthropic gets better has itself doubled.

What is driving the acceleration is the part that should hold your attention: AI itself. On METR’s account, much of the speed-up comes from the models doing the work — writing code, running experiments, testing and refining — with Anthropic staff reporting large productivity gains from working alongside them. In plain English, Claude is now a meaningful part of the team building the next Claude. The industry has predicted this loop for years and argued about when it would actually bite. An outside measurer has now put a number on it: about two times faster, with the machines supplying much of the push.

Then comes the catch, and it is a big one. METR also noted that Anthropic redacted most of the most informative evidence in its roughly 100-page assessment. The public can read the conclusions; it cannot inspect all the underlying measurements those conclusions rest on. That does not make the findings false — METR saw what it saw and signed its name to the summary. But it does mean the rest of us are being asked to take the single most consequential claim in AI right now — the frontier is accelerating, powered by itself — substantially on trust.

The timing sharpens everything. Anthropic has filed its S-1 publicly for a Nasdaq listing under the ticker ANT, which means public investors will soon be asked to price this company. The pitch writes itself: a growth curve bending upward, progress compounding at twice the old speed. The fine print is METR’s: the strongest evidence behind the bend in that curve is, for now, blacked out.

Why it matters: if progress is genuinely compounding at double pace, every plan built on last year’s assumptions — jobs, regulation, investment, safety timelines — is out of date on arrival. And if the proof sits behind redactions while the company sells shares to the public, “how fast is this moving?” becomes a question answered partly on faith, at exactly the moment the stakes for getting it wrong are highest.

Policy · MarketsNov. 7

Voters Just Repriced the AI Boom: OpenAI Promises to Pay for Its Own Power After Data-Center Backlash

The AI boom just received its first real bill from the voters. In this week’s US midterms, data centers were effectively on the ballot in roughly 40 localities — and by Wednesday, about 28 measures had passed across 25 of those localities opposing or limiting them. The campaigns were not fought over abstract fears about artificial intelligence. They were fought over electricity bills: households watching their power costs climb had connected the dots to the server farms moving in down the road, and they voted like it.

Two days later, the industry’s biggest name answered. OpenAI published a National Community Compact pledging to pay for the electricity and grid infrastructure for the campuses it owns and operates — and, crucially, to post financial guarantees before major spending, so that if a project is delayed or cancelled, other utility customers are not left holding the bill for grid upgrades built for a tenant who never arrived. In plain English: we pay our own way, and we put money down up front to prove it. It is a concession written in the only language the backlash was speaking — who pays.

The stock market had already delivered its own verdict. AI stocks plunged on Thursday: the Nasdaq fell 5.52% and roughly $1.2 trillion in value evaporated in a day, as investors repriced the boom around a risk that has nothing to do with chips or model quality — permission. The panic did not hold: Nvidia rebounded more than 5% on Friday morning, a reminder that the market is arguing with itself about whether the politics is a squall or a climate change.

Washington, meanwhile, is converting the mood into law. Democratic lawmakers are planning a package of AI and data-centre bills covering electricity costs, independent evaluation of models, and supplier liability, according to Reuters (November 6). The thread running through all three is accountability with a price tag: if AI raises the neighbourhood’s power bill, plugs into public grids, or causes harm, somebody identifiable pays — and it is not the ratepayer by default.

Why it matters: for three years, the binding constraint on AI was compute — chips, capital, engineers. This week it became consent. The models still have to plug in somewhere, somewhere just voted no in 25 localities, and the most powerful company in the field has now volunteered to pay for its own power before the law makes it. Expect every data-center pitch from here on to start with the electricity bill, not the ribbon-cutting.

Security · OpenAI — Lead storyNov. 6

OpenAI Unleashes GPT-6 Cyber — a Critical-Rated Hacking Model Locked Behind ID Checks

OpenAI just released the most capable hacking AI it has ever built — and wrapped it in more locks than any model before it. GPT-6 Cyber is live for a hand-picked few, and OpenAI itself rates it Critical.

The release rolled out across November 5–6, and it is deliberately not for everyone. GPT-6 Cyber is available only in limited access to vetted customers of Daybreak Red — OpenAI’s programme for professional defenders — through the API and Codex, its coding environment. There is no public sign-up and no casual experimenting. If you want this model, OpenAI wants to know exactly who you are first.

The reason is what it can do. On ExploitGym — a benchmark that tests whether an AI can turn a known security weakness into a working break-in — GPT-6 Cyber scored 48.5%, a huge jump from the 32.1% posted by its predecessor, GPT-5.6 Cyber. In plain English: given a flaw and a target, this model now builds a functioning exploit nearly half the time. That is not a chatbot trick. That is a working intrusion capability, improving by half again in a single generation.

The field results are bigger than the benchmark. GPT-6 Cyber reproduced 1,724 vulnerabilities in the Linux kernel — the core of the operating system that runs most of the world’s servers — and built working exploits for 948 of them. It also found 81 previously unknown high-severity flaws in Firefox, the browser millions rely on precisely because no single tech giant controls it. And OpenAI says it has disclosed 304 critical Linux findings to the people who maintain the code, complete with proposed patches — fixes drafted and ready for review.

OpenAI is treating its own creation accordingly. Under its Preparedness Framework — the company’s internal yardstick for how dangerous a model could be — GPT-6 Cyber is rated Critical for cybersecurity, the most serious classification in that category. Access requires identity verification, single sign-on, multi-factor authentication and IP allowlisting, meaning the model will only answer from approved network addresses. Alongside the launch, OpenAI announced a $1 billion initiative for the security professionals who defend critical infrastructure — the power grids, hospitals and water systems that can least afford to lose this race. It is priced like the serious tool it is: $20 per million input tokens and $100 per million output tokens, more expensive than the rival offerings from Anthropic and Google.

One honest caution before the headlines run away: every benchmark number here is OpenAI-reported and has not yet been independently confirmed. But even discounted for marketing, the direction is unmistakable.

Why it matters: yesterday’s story was an AI that could find flaws. Today’s is an AI that finds them, weaponises nearly a thousand of them, drafts the patches, and is sold only through a door with four locks on it. The capability is no longer the question. Who gets through the door is.

Safety · Evaluation — Lead storyNov. 6

Anthropic’s Progress Just Accelerated 1.9× — and METR Says AI Itself Did Most of It

An independent watchdog just measured something the AI world has only argued about: progress at Anthropic is not just fast — it is speeding up, and AI itself is doing most of the pushing.

METR — the independent evaluator that labs invite in to stress-test their models before release — published a high-access assessment of Anthropic on November 5. High-access means METR got an unusually deep look inside: not a public demo, but real evidence about how fast capability is actually moving. Its headline estimate: the pace of Anthropic’s benchmark capability progress has accelerated 1.9 times. Not improved — accelerated. The speed itself nearly doubled.

The estimate comes with an honest margin: a confidence interval of 1.5 to 2.8 times, meaning the true figure plausibly sits anywhere in that range. But the number that should stop you is the next one. METR attributes roughly 80% of the acceleration to AI itself — not to buying more computers, and not to hiring more people. In plain English: the models are now doing a large share of the work that makes the next models better. The flywheel the industry has talked about for years is, on this evidence, actually spinning.

METR is equally clear about what it could not see. Crucial evidence behind the assessment was redacted, and the evaluator warns that Anthropic’s own risk report may understate how close its models are to AI-accelerated self-improvement — the point where a system meaningfully speeds up the creation of its own successor. That is not an accusation of bad faith; it is a measurement warning. The company’s official map of the road ahead may show more distance than is really there.

Put the two halves together and you get the real story. The people charged with watching this technology from the outside are saying, with numbers attached, that progress is compounding faster than the watched company’s own documents suggest — and that part of the evidence needed to check the claim has been blacked out. Transparency is now lagging the very acceleration it is supposed to track.

Why it matters: every safety plan, every regulation and every hiring forecast in AI quietly assumes progress continues at roughly today’s pace. If the pace itself is compounding — and if the machines are the ones compounding it — those plans are built on yesterday’s slope. METR’s job is to say the quiet part early. It just did.

Models · GoogleNov. 6

Google’s Gemini Omni 1.2 Flash Cuts AI Video to 7 Cents a Second — Sound Included

Google DeepMind has released Gemini Omni 1.2 Flash, and it is available everywhere Google sells AI: on Google Cloud, through the Gemini API, in AI Studio for developers, and inside Google Vids, the company’s video creation tool. This is not a research preview behind a waitlist. It is a product, on the shelf, today.

What it makes is a complete little film. Gemini Omni generates synchronised audio and video together — 720p clips of up to 15 seconds where the sound is born with the picture, not pasted on afterwards. A door slams when the door slams. That sounds obvious until you remember that most AI video until now has been silent film with the audio bolted on later, usually slightly wrong. Fifteen seconds is short, but it is exactly the length of the clips that fill social feeds, product pages and ad slots — the formats where most video on the internet now lives.

The editing model is the other half of the release. Omni supports multi-turn iterative editing: you generate a clip, then keep talking to it — change the light, swap the setting, adjust the action — refining across a conversation instead of re-rolling the dice from scratch each time. Anyone who has generated AI video knows the pain of the almost-right clip that a fresh prompt destroys. This is Google’s answer: direction, not gambling.

Then there is the price, which may be the real headline. Output pricing drops about 30%, to roughly $0.07 per second of video, down from $0.10. A full 15-second clip now costs about a dollar in output. AI video is following the path AI text took: the demo phase is over, and the fight has moved to unit economics — who can sell a usable second of finished, sound-on video the cheapest.

Why it matters: video is the most expensive content humans make and the format the internet runs on. A tool that produces directed, editable, sound-synchronised clips for pennies a second does not just threaten stock footage — it reprices every small business that ever skipped video because a camera crew cost too much.

Markets · Data CentersNov. 6

The AI Trade Cracks: Nasdaq’s Worst Day in 19 Months as Voters Reject Data Centers 40 to 1

US stocks fell sharply on Thursday, and the damage was concentrated exactly where the boom has been. The Nasdaq dropped about 5.5% — its worst day since April, and its worst in 19 months by the broader measure — wiping trillions of dollars in market value in a single session. The trigger was not a bad earnings report. It was investors stepping back and reassessing the great AI data-centre buildout: the hundreds of billions being poured into server farms, and whether the politics underneath them still holds.

The politics, it turns out, is cracking. Democratic wins in the midterms shifted the mood in Washington, but the sharper signal came from much closer to the ground: voters rejected data centers in 40 of 41 local ballot measures. Forty out of forty-one. Communities that were supposed to compete for these projects instead voted, nearly everywhere, to keep them out — over power prices, water use, noise, and the suspicion that the benefits flow to distant shareholders while the costs stay local.

The industry heard it. OpenAI responded by publishing a data center policy with real concessions in it: the company commits to pay 100% of the electricity and infrastructure costs at sites it owns and operates, so local ratepayers do not subsidise its training runs; to end the non-disclosure agreements it has signed with public officials, which kept deals secret from the people living next door; and to publish per-site water and power use, so every community can see exactly what a facility takes.

Google is making the same calculation at full scale. The company is committing $17 billion to a data center in Wyoming — and pledging to cover its power costs as part of the deal. Two of the biggest builders in the business, arriving at the same conclusion in the same week: the era of quietly landing a mega-facility and letting the town absorb the costs is over.

Why it matters: for three years the AI race was limited by chips and capital. This week it ran into a harder limit — consent. The models can be as smart as they like; they still have to plug in somewhere, and somewhere just voted no, forty times over.

Models · Open WeightsNov. 6

Mira Murati’s Inkling 2 Small Packs 594 Billion Parameters — and Wakes Only 17 Billion of Them

Thinking Machines Lab, the company founded by former OpenAI chief technology officer Mira Murati, has released Inkling 2 Small — and the name is doing heavy irony. By the numbers this is one of the largest models ever announced: 594 billion parameters in total. But it is a mixture-of-experts model, which means it works like a newsroom of specialists rather than one giant brain: for any given question, only the relevant experts wake up. In Inkling’s case that is just 17 billion active parameters per query — roughly 3% of the whole — which is where the “Small” earns its keep: big-model knowledge at a fraction of the running cost.

The performance backs the design. Inkling 2 Small scores 31 on the Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index, the independent scoreboard that blends reasoning, knowledge and agentic tests into a single number. That score is described as the highest ever posted by an open-weights model developed outside China — with one asterisk, which the next paragraph handles. The closed American labs still lead overall, but the strongest Western open challenger now comes from a lab barely old enough to have a track record.

The asterisk: for now, the weights are not out. Inkling 2 Small runs only through Tinker, Thinking Machines’ own platform, with the model itself closed. The open release is planned for next week under the Apache 2.0 licence — the permissive licence that lets anyone download, modify and build products on a model — after external safety testing and red-teaming are complete. In other words, independent experts get to attack it first, and only then does everyone else get the keys.

That sequencing is worth noticing. Open-weights releases have often been a race to publish first and answer questions later. Thinking Machines is doing the opposite: hosted access now, outside scrutiny next, full release after. It is a small lab making a deliberate argument that openness and caution can ship in the same box.

Why it matters: open weights are where AI becomes infrastructure — models companies can own outright instead of renting by the token. If Inkling’s Apache 2.0 release lands next week as promised, the open ecosystem outside China gets its strongest foundation yet, from a lab that chose to let red-teamers shoot at it before handing it to the world.

Sources: Thinking Machines Lab · Artificial Analysis · Nov. 6, 2026

Models · Open Model — Lead storyNov. 5

Tencent Drops Hy4: A 770-Billion-Parameter Open Model That Sees, Clicks, and Challenges OpenAI and Anthropic

Tencent just put a 770-billion-parameter model on the table that can read a chart, do visual math, and then reach out and use the computer itself — and it is aimed squarely at the closed labs.

The scale comes first. Hy4, launched November 4, carries 770 billion total parameters — the full library of what the model knows — but wakes up only about 49 billion of them for any single query. That is the mixture-of-experts trick that has become the industry’s standard bargain: frontier size without frontier running costs on every question. Its memory is just as outsized: up to 1 million tokens of context, enough to hold whole codebases, long contracts or a small library of documents in a single conversation.

What is genuinely new is the range. Hy4 is the first Hunyuan model with real image understanding — not just describing a picture, but reading charts and working through visual math, the kind of diagram-heavy problems that trip up text-only systems. And it does not stop at looking. Hy4 can use a computer directly, working through a graphical interface and the command line: clicking, typing and running tools the way a person at a keyboard would. That moves it out of the chatbot category and into the agent category, where the product is finished work, not replies.

Tencent is not being modest about where it lands. The company says Hy4 is comparable to Moonshot’s Kimi K3.1 Pro and beats Z.ai’s GLM-5.3 and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash in its own benchmarks. Read those as vendor claims until independent testing lands — but the pricing underneath them is concrete: around $0.90 per million input tokens and $2.70 per million output tokens, a fraction of what the top American closed models charge for comparable work.

One accuracy point matters here. Tencent says it intends to release Hy4 under Apache 2.0, with weights and safety and system cards promised in the coming days — they were not yet released at launch. “Open” is the plan and the direction, not yet a download link. What is already live is the product: Hy4 is powering WorkBuddy and Tencent’s cloud apps now, so customers are using it while the open release catches up. Tencent also says the model ships with safeguards aimed at cyber and biological misuse — the standard, necessary fine print for a system that can operate a computer.

Why it matters: the open-model race is no longer about catching up on chat. Hy4 pairs enormous scale with eyes and hands — vision in, actions out — at open-model prices. If the promised weights land under Apache 2.0, every developer on Earth gets a computer-using agent they can actually own, and OpenAI and Anthropic have to argue, again, for why renting beats holding.

Sources: Tencent · Nov. 5, 2026
Business · IPO Filing — Lead storyNov. 5

Anthropic’s $17 Billion Quarter — And an $895 Billion Compute Bill

Anthropic just reported the biggest quarter in AI history — $17 billion in three months. In the same filing, it promised to spend $895 billion. Both numbers are real. Only one of them has to keep growing.

Start with the good news, because it is genuinely astonishing. Anthropic’s amended S-1, filed November 2, shows $16.96 billion — call it $17 billion — in revenue in the third quarter alone, up about 47% on the second quarter. Over the last four quarters the company has brought in roughly $35 billion. A few years ago Anthropic was a research lab with a chatbot. It is now generating revenue at a pace most Fortune 500 companies never reach.

Now the costs, which are growing faster than the money coming in. Operating expenses jumped from $10.6 billion to $17.5 billion, and an operating profit flipped into an operating loss. In plain English: Anthropic is now spending more than a dollar for every dollar it earns, on purpose, because in this race standing still is the same as falling behind. The profit will come later, the strategy says — if later arrives on schedule.

Then there is the number that dwarfs everything else in the filing: $895.4 billion in commitments. Nearly a trillion dollars, promised in advance, and nearly all of it is compute — the chips, the electricity and the buildings needed to train and run Claude for years to come. One piece shows how deep the web runs: Google is backstopping $19 billion of Anthropic’s lease at a 401-megawatt campus in Kentucky tied to Google TPUs. A megawatt powers roughly a few hundred homes; 401 megawatts is a small city, dedicated to one tenant’s AI — with Google standing behind the rent.

So what does it mean to bet nearly a trillion dollars against $35 billion a year in revenue? It means Anthropic has promised to spend about twenty-five years of its current annual revenue, in advance, on the assumption that revenue keeps compounding at something like its current pace. If AI demand keeps doubling, those commitments look visionary — capacity bought early is capacity rivals cannot have. If growth slows to merely fast, the bills do not slow with it. Leases, chips and power contracts are fixed promises in a business whose income is not.

Why it matters: this is the whole AI boom in one filing. The revenue is real and historic; so is the wager. Anthropic is not just selling AI — it is pre-paying for a future in which AI demand never blinks, and the $895 billion question hanging over the entire industry is what happens on the first quarter it does.

Sources: Anthropic S-1/A filing · SEC · Nov. 5, 2026
Security · AgentsNov. 5

OpenAI Let a Cyber Agent Loose on Linux — It Found 1,724 Real Vulnerabilities

OpenAI pointed a prototype cyber agent at one of the most scrutinised codebases on Earth — roughly 2.8 million lines of a hardened desktop Linux kernel, software that has been poked at by expert human hackers for decades — and let it hunt. Within 12 hours it had produced 2,308 candidate findings. Within 30 hours, 1,724 of them had been reproduced as real vulnerabilities. Not suspicions. Confirmed flaws, in hardened code, found by a machine in a day and a quarter.

The agent did not stop at pointing. For 948 of the bugs — local privilege-escalation flaws, the kind that let an ordinary user seize full control of a machine — it produced working proof-of-concept exploits and candidate patches. It demonstrated each break-in and then drafted the fix. The entire run cost $1.21 million in API bills, which works out to about $1,279 per bug. OpenAI estimates the same haul would be worth $18.9 million to $66.3 million at bug-bounty rates. The economics of finding flaws just changed by an order of magnitude.

This is now a product, with a gate on it. On November 4, OpenAI made Daybreak Red, running GPT-5.6 Cyber, available to eligible, approved defensive security practitioners through Amazon Bedrock on AWS GovCloud US-East — the government-grade cloud — subject to separate approval. In plain English: vetted defenders can rent the hunter, but you cannot simply sign up with a credit card.

OpenAI is not the only lab whose AI is finding the cracks. The Xen Project — the open-source hypervisor that keeps virtual machines separated on shared servers — has credited Anthropic’s Claude with discovering two critical VM-escape flaws, the kind that let software break out of its sandbox and reach the host. When both leading labs’ models are surfacing critical bugs in core infrastructure, it stops being a demo and starts being a shift in how security work gets done.

Here is the honest tension, in plain English. The same skill that finds a flaw to patch it can find a flaw to exploit it — the agent does not know which team it is on; its operator decides. Defence gets cheaper and faster at exactly the rate offence does. What tips the balance is access and speed: gates like Daybreak Red’s approvals, and defenders patching at machine pace instead of human pace.

Why it matters: for thirty years, finding a serious vulnerability took a rare expert weeks. An agent just found 1,724 of them in 30 hours for about $1,279 each. Every piece of important software on Earth is about to be audited by machines — the only question is whose machines get there first.

Sources: OpenAI · Xen Project · Nov. 5, 2026
Products · OpenAINov. 5

OpenAI Images 2.6 Brings GIFs to ChatGPT — And OpenAI Buys Pierre to Rebuild Code for Agents

Two announcements landed from OpenAI on November 4, and they belong in the same story. First: Images 2.6 is live in ChatGPT, Codex and the API, and the headline feature is motion — animated GIFs, generated on request, alongside reference images that let you steer a picture by showing the model an example. Generation is 50% faster. The scale underneath is hard to picture: OpenAI says people now create 4 billion images a week in its products. Four billion. Still images were the warm-up act; now the pictures move.

Second: OpenAI said it will acquire Pierre Computer Company. Pierre is not a household name, but programmers know its work — Code Storage, and the Trees and Diffs libraries that already sit inside Codex, OpenAI’s coding agent. Diffs are how software shows you what changed between two versions of code; trees are how it organises the files. They sound like plumbing because they are — and plumbing is exactly the point of the deal.

To see why, you have to understand what Git is. Git is the source-control system nearly every programmer on Earth uses to track changes, collaborate and undo mistakes. It was designed in 2005 for humans: humans who write a little code, review each other’s changes line by line, and commit work in tidy, deliberate steps. Coding agents do none of that. An agent can rewrite thousands of lines in minutes, try fifty approaches in parallel and throw forty-nine away. Pouring that flood through tools built for a trickle is already creaking — so OpenAI is buying the plumbers to rebuild source control around how agents actually work.

Now put the two announcements side by side and the pattern snaps into focus. Images going from still to animated is AI moving from making things to making things that move, loop and communicate — a GIF is not an answer, it is a piece of media someone publishes. Git being rebuilt for agents is AI moving from suggesting code to shipping it, at volumes human workflows were never designed to hold. Different products, same direction of travel.

Why it matters: the first era of AI was a machine that answered. The era arriving now is a machine that produces and ships — the meme, the animation, the finished feature, the merged pull request. OpenAI spent one day upgrading both ends of that pipeline: what AI makes, and the roads its work travels on.

Science · ClaudeNov. 5

Claude Just Helped Solve a Biological Structure No One Had Cracked

Some structures in biology sit unsolved for years because they are too big, too symmetrical and too fragile to pin down. Anthropic reported on November 4 that one of them has fallen — with Claude’s help. Researchers produced a cryo-EM structure of a vault-like particle derived from a bacteriophage, built from 96 copies of the same protein locked together, described as the first prokaryotic vault particle structure ever solved.

Plain English, piece by piece. A bacteriophage is a virus that infects bacteria — nature’s own tiny syringe, harmless to us. A vault is a barrel-shaped container that cells build out of protein, like a microscopic cargo pod with doors; scientists have wanted to understand vaults for decades because a hollow, programmable container is exactly what you would design if you wanted to deliver medicine to a precise spot in the body. Prokaryotic simply means it comes from the bacterial world — this vault’s design was borrowed from a phage, and nobody had ever mapped one like it.

Cryo-EM is how you photograph something far too small for any camera. Samples are frozen so fast the water turns to glass instead of ice, then an electron microscope takes thousands of two-dimensional snapshots of individual particles from different angles, and computers average them into one sharp three-dimensional map — in this case, resolving how all 96 protein copies fit together. Claude’s role was the unglamorous, essential middle of the work: assisting with sequence searches to find the right protein relatives, purification planning to isolate the particle cleanly, and analysis of the microscopy data itself.

Why does a 96-copy particle matter? Because symmetry is a blueprint. If 96 identical parts self-assemble into a precise container, scientists can learn the assembly rules — and then, in principle, engineer containers of their own: pods that carry a drug, protect it on the journey and open at the destination. That is a long road, and honesty matters here: these findings still need laboratory validation. A structure, however beautiful, is a map — not yet a medicine.

Claude was not the only AI doing bench science this week. Google DeepMind reported that its Antigravity chemistry skills proposed macrocycles — large ring-shaped molecules prized in drug design because rings can grip targets that floppy molecules cannot — in about an hour. Same caveat, stated plainly: those proposals still require synthesis and lab validation before they are chemistry rather than computation.

Why it matters: the pattern is bigger than one particle. AI is moving from answering questions about science to doing the slow middle hours of science — searching, planning, analysing — at a pace no lab could staff. The validation still belongs to humans in wet labs, exactly as it should. But the map drawer just got much faster.

Sources: Anthropic · Google DeepMind · Nov. 5, 2026

Business · IPO Filing — Lead storyNov. 4

Anthropic’s IPO Filing Drops a Bombshell: $33 Billion in Revenue — and a Warning AI May Soon Improve Itself

Anthropic’s amended IPO filing puts a number on the AI boom that is hard to say out loud — $33.24 billion in revenue in nine months — and then, in the risk factors, quietly warns that AI may soon start improving itself.

The headline number is $33.24 billion: Anthropic’s revenue for the first nine months of 2026, disclosed in an amended S-1 filing as the company prepares to go public. The third quarter alone brought in $17 billion — more than half the nine-month total in a single quarter. That is up 1,127% from the same quarter a year earlier, a growth rate that would be extraordinary for a startup selling lemonade, let alone a company selling AI to the largest enterprises on Earth.

The growth is cooling, though, and the filing is honest about it. Quarter-over-quarter growth slowed to 47%, down from 144% the quarter before — still blistering by any normal standard, but the curve is bending. What is not bending is the spending. Anthropic disclosed roughly $895.4 billion in purchase and compute commitments: close to a trillion dollars of promised future buying, the price of staying in a race where the entry ticket is a small country’s power grid.

That grid is the next number. Anthropic aims to roughly double its computing capacity next year, from about 3.5 gigawatts to about 7 gigawatts. In plain terms, a gigawatt is roughly the output of one large power plant — Anthropic plans to add the equivalent of several, in a year, just to train and run Claude.

Then comes the sentence that will be quoted for years. In its risk factors, Anthropic says that by its own internal estimates it “may soon” reach the threshold in its Responsible Scaling Policy for significant AI-driven acceleration of progress. Translated from filing-speak: the point where AI itself starts driving AI progress — models helping design, train and improve the next generation of models, in a loop that feeds on itself. Researchers call it recursive self-improvement, and for decades it was the stuff of thought experiments. Anthropic just put it in an SEC document.

The filing offers a glimpse of why the company thinks the loop is close. Anthropic disclosed that in September 2026, Claude wrote over 90% of its code contributions. The tool is already building the toolmaker. When the thing writing your software is your own product, every improvement compounds: a better Claude writes better code, which trains a better Claude.

Why it matters: IPO filings are legal documents, written to protect a company from being sued for what it left out — which makes the self-improvement warning the opposite of hype. Anthropic is telling investors two things at once: the money is real and enormous, and the technology may be approaching the moment it starts accelerating itself. Both can be true. The filing says they are.

Business · ChatGPT — Lead storyNov. 4

ChatGPT Just Hit 1.2 Billion Weekly Users — and Now Does 20% of Google’s Search Volume

OpenAI says ChatGPT now reaches 1.2 billion people every week — and that the questions they ask it add up to a fifth of all Google searches. The search era is quietly ending.

One point two billion. That is how many people now use ChatGPT every week, according to OpenAI — up from 1 billion roughly two months ago, a gain of 200 million weekly users in about eight weeks. For scale, that is adding a user base larger than most social networks ever reach, four times a year. ChatGPT is no longer a product people try; it is infrastructure people assume, like email or maps.

How they use it is shifting, too. Voice input and dictation now top 250 million weekly users — a quarter of a billion people who would rather talk than type. That matters because talking is how people ask real questions: longer, messier, more honest. A typed search is a keyword; a spoken question is a thought. Whoever owns the interface where the thought lands owns the answer that follows.

And here is the number that should keep Google awake. OpenAI says ChatGPT’s search-like queries now equal roughly 20% of Google’s total search volume. One in five. Google built the most profitable business in internet history on being the place questions go — and a fifth of that flow now runs through a chatbot that returns an answer instead of ten blue links. Every query that ends in ChatGPT is a query that never sees an ad, never clicks a publisher, never feeds the machine that funded the open web.

OpenAI is tightening the product around the scale. It has doubled usage limits for Pro subscribers, and it is moving ChatGPT and Codex — its coding agent — onto a single shared usage pool, so subscribers draw chat and code from the same allowance. That merger is a rehearsal: in December, OpenAI plans to fold Chat and Work together, blurring the line between asking the AI a question and handing it a job.

Why it matters: at 1.2 billion weekly users, ChatGPT’s design choices — what it answers, what it cites, what it refuses — shape what a meaningful slice of humanity believes. Google spent two decades as the front door to the world’s information. The door now has a second, very crowded entrance, and the building behind it is being rebuilt around answers, not links.

Platforms · AgentsNov. 4

Apple Opens Siri AI to Every App — While Warning Investors Agents Could Go Rogue

Apple just opened the biggest door in consumer AI. Through App Intents in the new iOS, iPadOS, macOS, watchOS and visionOS 27.3 betas — rolling out this week in six languages — developers can connect their apps’ content and actions directly to Siri AI. That means Siri stops being a voice that answers questions and starts being an agent that does things: not just “where is my package,” but checking the order in one app and arranging the delivery in another, in a single multi-step request.

The reach is difficult to overstate. Siri already fields about 2.5 billion requests a day. Even a small fraction of those turning into real actions — reordering, booking, rescheduling, paying — would make Apple, overnight, the largest distributor of AI agents on the planet, on devices already in a billion pockets.

Now read the other document Apple published this week. In its FY2026 10-K annual filing, Apple added a new risk warning about autonomous AI agents — and it is unusually blunt for a legal filing. Agents, Apple warns, could exceed their permissions, resist instructions, complete unauthorized transactions, access systems they are not authorized to reach, delete data, or conceal their own actions. And the consequences, the company adds, could be difficult to reverse.

Put the two side by side and you get the honest picture of where agents are in late 2026. The same company handing Siri the keys to every app on your phone is telling its own investors, in writing, that software acting on your behalf can go off the rails in ways that cannot simply be undone. That is not hypocrisy; it is the actual engineering problem, stated plainly. An agent useful enough to act across your apps is, by definition, powerful enough to act wrongly across them.

There is a geopolitical layer, too. In China, Apple Intelligence — Siri, Writing Tools and Visual Intelligence — will be powered by Alibaba’s Qwen models, with developer testing starting November 4 and a public launch planned for December on iPhone 15 Pro and newer. One Apple Intelligence, two engines, split by regulation.

Why it matters: Apple opening Siri to every app is the moment agents go mainstream — billions of requests a day meeting software that can finally act on them. Apple’s own filing is the fine print on that future: the more an assistant can do for you, the more damage it can do to you, and “difficult to reverse” is the phrase to remember.

Policy · EducationNov. 4

‘That’s Extortion’: Bernie Sanders Slams Google for Tying School Search to AI

Senator Bernie Sanders picked a fight on November 4, and he did not pick his words carefully — he picked them to sting. Reacting to a New York Times report that schools which reject AI entirely could lose access to Google Search, Sanders posted: “Google is threatening schools: if you stop your students from using AI, we will cut off your access to Google Search. That’s not love. That’s extortion.” He followed it with three more words: “PAUSE AI NOW.”

He was not alone. New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani accused Google of using monopoly power to force AI into public schools, and former FTC Chair Lina Khan — the antitrust enforcer who spent years in open conflict with Big Tech — criticized the policy as well. When a democratic socialist senator, a big-city mayor and the country’s most famous trust-buster land on the same target in one news cycle, a company has a political problem, not a messaging problem.

Google answered with a blog post, “Designing responsible AI for learning,” and posts on X. Its case: Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom and Search are off by default, and “schools are in control.” Google also promised that AI Mode and AI Overviews in education will lean on Guided Learning — step-by-step help that walks a student toward understanding instead of handing over quick answers. It is a reasonable-sounding defence, carefully built around the classroom experience.

But notice what it does not say. In its first response, Google did not directly address the core of the reporting: whether a school that refuses AI outright actually keeps its Search access. “Off by default” answers a question nobody asked. The question on the table is what happens to the school that says no to all of it — and until Google answers that one sentence plainly, the word “extortion” will keep doing its work.

The fight also predates this week. Senators Richard Blumenthal and Josh Hawley — a Democrat and a Republican who agree on little — had already been investigating Google’s push of AI into classrooms. Google’s tools are the default stack in much of American public education, which is exactly why the stakes are high: when one company supplies the Chromebooks, the classroom software and the search box, “just switch” is not a real option for a school district.

Why it matters: this is the first major political battle over AI in the classroom, and it is really about leverage. Search is infrastructure schools cannot easily replace; AI is the product Google most wants adopted. Whether or not Google intended the tie, two senators were already watching — and now the loudest voice in American politics on corporate power has given the fight a one-word headline.

China’s Meituan Drops a 1.6-Trillion-Parameter Model Trained Entirely on Chinese Chips — for $0.30 per Million Tokens

The biggest model of the month comes from a food-delivery company, and it is priced like a dare. On November 2, China’s Meituan released LongCat-2.5, a native multimodal model with 1.6 trillion total parameters — about 48 billion active on any given task — trained entirely on domestic Chinese chips and accelerators. No American silicon in the loop. Export controls were supposed to make exactly this kind of run impossible; Meituan did it anyway.

The design brief is work, not chat. LongCat-2.5 is built for long-horizon agent tasks — jobs where a model has to plan, use tools and keep going for hours — and for agentic coding, with mid-training sequences stretched up to 1 million tokens so it can hold enormous context: whole codebases, long contracts, entire project histories. This release also adds native image understanding, so vision is part of the model rather than a bolt-on. A preview version allowed outputs of up to 128,000 tokens — a short book in a single reply.

Then there is the price. LongCat-2.5 launches at $0.30 per million input tokens and $1.20 per million output tokens — pennies next to frontier American pricing, cheap enough that a developer can run serious agent workloads for less than the cost of the engineer’s coffee. Meituan says open releases on GitHub and Hugging Face will follow, which would put the weights in anyone’s hands, but it gave no date.

Now the cold water, courtesy of SemiAnalysis. Its new GDPx benchmark puts agent models through 206 real professional tasks — the multi-hour knowledge work agents are supposed to take over — and the best-scoring model managed 12.0%. Twelve percent. The models are getting bigger, cheaper and more ambitious at a dizzying pace, and on work that resembles an actual job, the best of them still fails seven times out of eight.

Why it matters: LongCat-2.5 proves two things at once. China’s AI industry can now train frontier-scale models on its own hardware and sell them at prices that undercut everyone — a strategic fact Washington will not ignore. And GDPx proves the gap between demo and dependable is still wide. Cheap and huge is here. Reliable is the race that actually counts.

Models · Open Weights — Lead storyNov. 3

Mistral Large 4 Goes Open Weight: Europe Drops a 1-Trillion-Parameter Model Anyone Can Download

The weights are out. Mistral has published Mistral Large 4 on Hugging Face for anyone to download, fine-tune and build on — roughly a trillion parameters of frontier AI, trained in Europe, and now the largest open-weight model ever released by a Western lab.

Start with the download itself, because that is the story. Mistral Large 4 is a Mixture-of-Experts model: about one trillion total parameters, of which roughly 49 billion are active on any given token — 52 billion if you count embeddings and the output layer. Only a slice of the brain wakes up for each job, which is how a model this size stays runnable at all. It is natively multimodal, handling more than text, and it ships under a modified MIT licence — the Mistral Ecosystem Open-Weight License.

The model was built the hard way. Mistral says Large 4 was trained from scratch on 3,800 Nvidia Grace Blackwell GPUs inside its own European data centres — no borrowed weights, no rented American cloud. The public first met it on October 6 as an API-only preview. What changed since is time: Mistral gave the model roughly three more weeks of reinforcement learning before opening the weights, so today’s download is a more polished model than the one reviewers previewed last month.

On performance, Mistral is swinging hard — and readers should know these are the company’s own claims, with independent tests still pending. Mistral says Large 4 leads open-weight models on coding benchmarks, tops open models on the Artificial Analysis Coding Agent Index, and ranks first among open-weight models on both FrontierCode 2.0 and Terminal-Bench 4.0. It also claims the model does its work using 47% fewer tokens than rivals — the same answers for a little over half the running cost, if the numbers hold up.

The practical details matter too. The release ships as Mistral-Large-4-1T-A52B in 8-bit FP8, with a smaller NVFP4 version tuned for Blackwell GPUs. The licence is permissive for most people: free to use, free to fine-tune, and free to build commercial products on top of. The one big exception is the business model itself — if you want to sell Large 4 as a hosted service, you need a separate commercial licence from Mistral. The model is open; the toll booth on reselling it is not.

Context sharpens the move. Large 4 lands just weeks after Meta’s 816-billion-parameter Muse Spark Open, and Mistral is pitching it on sovereignty and security: a frontier model a government, bank or hospital can hold inside its own walls, on European infrastructure, with no data leaving the building. In a market where the biggest American labs rent intelligence by the token, Mistral sold the deed.

Why it matters: open weights turn AI from a subscription into property — control, predictable cost and data sovereignty in one download. If Large 4’s benchmark claims survive independent testing, every closed lab now has to explain why renting a model beats owning one this strong.

Sources: Mistral AI · Hugging Face · Nov. 3, 2026
Safety · IPO Filing — Lead storyNov. 3

Anthropic Warns in Its IPO Filing That AI May Soon Start Improving Itself Faster Than Humans Can Check It

Buried in the Risk Factors of Anthropic’s amended IPO filing is a sentence no AI company has ever had to write before: its own AI may soon start getting better faster than the company can check that it is safe.

The language arrived Monday, November 2, in an amended S-1/A filing with the SEC, and was first flagged by TechCrunch. Anthropic told prospective investors that its increasing use of AI to accelerate its own research and development may cause model capabilities to advance more rapidly than its ability to develop, validate and implement safety, alignment and security measures. Read that again slowly: the bottleneck is no longer building the AI. It is checking it.

Then comes the threshold. Anthropic says its internal estimates for future models — not current ones — indicate it may imminently meet its own Responsible Scaling Policy definition of “dramatically accelerated progress attributable to AI.” That is the company’s formal term for recursive self-improvement: the point where AI progress is being driven mainly by AI itself, each generation helping to build a smarter next one, in a loop that compounds.

The filing is equally careful about what it is not saying. Anthropic has not designated any current model as crossing that threshold, and it says it is still assessing the data. It also concedes an uncomfortable history: in the past, the company has not identified a significant acceleration until after it had already occurred. In plain English, the smoke detector has previously gone off after the fire was already burning.

The numbers behind the warning explain why it is credible. In September 2026, Claude authored more than 90% of the contributions to Anthropic’s own codebase — the AI is, quite literally, writing the software company. And average daily code output per engineer in the third quarter was more than 45 times its 2024 level. When your engineers are 45 times as productive because the model is doing the work, the model’s next version arrives on a schedule set by the model.

Anthropic’s stated guardrail is human supervision. The company commits that it does not anticipate being able to safely use Claude to fully autonomously design and build successive generations of frontier models without meaningful human oversight, and it says it is considering updates to its Responsible Scaling Policy specifically to keep humans in control of that loop. Whether “meaningful” survives contact with a 45-times productivity curve is the open question.

Why it matters: IPO filings are legal documents, not blog posts — companies write them to protect themselves, which makes this one read less like hype and more like a disclosure. The lab building Claude is telling Wall Street, in writing, that self-improving AI is close enough to plan around.

Trust · App StoresNov. 3

400 Innocent-Looking AI Apps Were Hiding Deepfake ‘Nudify’ Tools in Plain Sight

The pet app dressed your dog in a little sweater. It could also, if the right switch flipped on a server far away, turn a photo of a real person into a sexual deepfake. A WIRED investigation published November 2, built on research by the nonprofit AI Forensics and the security firm Infoblox, has mapped a network of more than 400 linked apps and websites — almost 500 connected domains — hiding deepfake “nudify” tools behind some of the most innocent software in the app stores.

The disguises were chosen to be boring. Pet-photo editors like Viefur, which dresses pets in human clothes. Language tutors. Workout coaches. A hair-styling app called Prilos. Nothing that would make a reviewer look twice, and that was the point: each app presented a clean, ordinary face to Apple, to Google and to most of the people who downloaded it.

The trick was a server-side switch. Depending on factors like the user’s location, language or device ID — or whether they installed the app after tapping a Meta ad — the same harmless-looking app would quietly load a second, hidden interface: tools for generating sexual and violent deepfakes from uploaded photos of real people. Victims never consented; customers paid for access in credits, bought through Apple’s and Google’s own payment systems, which means the platforms took their usual cut of a business they did not know existed.

The scale was not trivial. In September, 27 of the apps were live on Google Play with more than 350,000 combined downloads, and one, DiffAI, had passed 100,000 downloads on its own. When WIRED came calling, the response split two ways: Apple removed 19 apps after being contacted and said the apps were clean when reviewed, while Google had already removed most of its share before WIRED reached out.

WIRED’s reporting points to Chinese developers and a Hong Kong-registered company, KeLing Technology Limited. Meta says it banned KeLing from advertising back in 2025 and has acted against thousands of related ad accounts. The rulebook is also catching up: Apple updated its guidelines on October 16–17, adding rule 2.5.19 against hidden features and bait-and-switch apps, and the European Union’s ban on nudifier apps takes effect in about a month, on December 2.

Why it matters: app review was built to catch what an app does on the day it is checked. A server-side switch means the app an investigator approves and the app a victim opens can be two different products — and 350,000 downloads say the gap is not theoretical.

Sources: WIRED · AI Forensics · Infoblox · Nov. 3, 2026
Policy · ElectionsNov. 3

Election Day War Rooms: OpenAI, Anthropic and Meta Brace for AI Misinformation as America Votes

America votes today, and for the first time, the biggest AI companies are spending Election Day the way campaigns do: in a war room. For the U.S. midterm elections on Tuesday, November 3, OpenAI and Anthropic have set up dedicated election operations centres to monitor for misuse and misleading voting information, according to reporting by Politico and Semafor via Nextgov. Meta has activated its own Election Operations Center as well. It is the first time OpenAI and Anthropic have deployed units like these in this form.

Anthropic described the posture plainly: its teams work year-round on election safeguards, and ahead of the election it has a dedicated war room monitoring for misuse and ready to respond quickly. That is the model borrowed from cybersecurity and campaign politics alike — specialists watching live feeds of prompts, images and viral claims, with the power to act in minutes rather than news cycles.

The reason for the urgency is a shift in where voters get answers. More people now ask a chatbot where to vote, when polls close or what a ballot measure means — which makes the chatbot part of the election infrastructure, errors included. The failure modes are already visible: deepfake images that spread faster than fact-checks, and plain AI search mistakes, like Google AI Mode recently declining to answer who won the 2020 election.

The defences are uneven, and we know that from testing. On October 22, the Brennan Center ran six AI image generators through election scenarios and found ChatGPT had significantly improved its protections, returning cartoonish images instead of realistic fakes. Several other platforms in the same test remained willing to generate realistic fake election imagery. A war room can catch misuse at scale; it cannot fix a rival’s looser guardrails.

The companies have also tried to ground their answers in official data. OpenAI, Anthropic and Meta have integrated voting information from Democracy Works, which compiles polling places, deadlines and procedures from state and local election officials — so a chatbot asked about voting is, ideally, reading from the same source as the county clerk rather than improvising from the internet.

Why it matters: elections now run on two information systems at once — the official one, and the one voters actually ask questions of. The war rooms are an admission that the second system is now big enough to need defending like the first.

Meituan’s LongCat-2.5: A 1.6-Trillion-Parameter Model Trained Entirely on Homegrown Chips

The biggest model announcement of the week did not come from a lab. It came from a food-delivery company. On Monday, Meituan — the Chinese on-demand giant behind everything from takeout to local services — unveiled LongCat-2.5, a native multimodal model with 1.6 trillion total parameters and about 48 billion activated per token. That puts it, on raw scale, beyond every open model released this month, from a company most Western readers know, if at all, for delivering lunch.

It is multimodal from the ground up, so text and images are native inputs rather than bolted-on features. In plain terms: less a chatbot, more a tireless digital employee aimed at real operational workloads.

The detail that will travel furthest in Washington is the hardware. LongCat-2.5 was trained entirely on homegrown Chinese chips — not a single top-tier American accelerator in the loop. Export controls were supposed to slow exactly this kind of frontier training run. A 1.6-trillion-parameter model trained without them is a proof point that China’s AI industry is learning to route around hardware limits rather than wait for them to lift.

Access starts inside Meituan’s own walls: LongCat-2.5 is available through the company’s online chat and API services, with releases on GitHub and Hugging Face planned. If those open releases land as promised, the model will not stay a Chinese curiosity for long — developers everywhere have a habit of adopting whatever is biggest, capable and downloadable.

Why it matters: the AI race is often told as a contest between labs. LongCat-2.5 is a reminder it is also a contest between systems — chips, ecosystems and business models — and a delivery giant just showed it can play at the very top of the scale chart.

Models · Open Weights — Lead storyNov. 2

Mistral Drops the Weights for “Le Chonk” — a Trillion Parameters, Free to Download

Mistral has publicly released the weights for Mistral Large 4 — a trillion-parameter open model the French lab nicknames “Le Chonk” — so anyone can download it and run it themselves. The pitch is not chatbots. It is sovereign cyber defense, and it just escalated the open-weight fight with the closed labs.

Start with what actually happened. Mistral, the French AI company, has put the weights of its biggest model out in public. “Weights” is the part that matters: not a demo behind a login, but the model itself, available via Hugging Face and Mistral’s own API, for businesses and governments to download, host and run on their own terms. A trillion parameters is frontier scale by any measure — and Mistral just handed the keys to everyone.

The job it is selling the model for is specific: sovereign cyber defense. Large 4 is pitched at helping organisations identify and fix cybersecurity vulnerabilities — the kind of work where the code, and the flaws hiding in it, are the last things you want to send to somebody else’s cloud. A government or a company that runs the model itself keeps its vulnerabilities in-house. That is the whole case for open weights in one sentence: your most sensitive problems never leave your building.

Mistral is not being shy about performance, either. The company says Large 4 matches GLM-5.3, the Chinese open-weight rival, particularly on cyber tasks — while using 47% fewer tokens per task, which is the same work for a little over half the running cost. And on Artificial Analysis’ Coding Agent Index, Mistral says Large 4 leads all open models for agentic coding — the multi-step, tool-using kind of coding work that is fast becoming the industry’s main event.

One detail worth knowing before you judge the release: the model out today is not the one unveiled earlier this month. It is an upgraded version of it, revised after testing with governments and corporate users. In other words, exactly the customers most likely to deploy it for defense work have already kicked the tires, and what shipped publicly is the improved cut.

Why it matters: the deepest split in AI is no longer which model is smartest — it is who physically holds the model. OpenAI, Anthropic and Google’s Gemini are mainly reached through online services the labs control. Mistral’s answer is a trillion-parameter model you can possess, run and defend yourself with. Every release like this makes the closed labs argue harder for why renting is better — and makes that argument harder to win.

Business · PricingNov. 2

ChatGPT and Codex Will Drink From the Same Well — OpenAI Merges Your Token Budget

Here is the change, in plain terms. Today, a paid subscription quietly keeps ChatGPT usage and Codex usage in separate buckets, each with its own quota. Starting in December 2026, OpenAI is merging the buckets. Plus, Pro and Business subscribers will draw ChatGPT and Codex from a single shared token allowance, replacing the separate product quotas entirely. Chat with the model all morning, then set Codex loose on a codebase all afternoon — every token comes out of the same pot.

That sounds like bookkeeping until you think about how differently the two products burn tokens. A conversation is cheap and bursty: short questions, short answers, done. A coding agent is neither. It can chew through tokens for as long as a task takes — reading, writing, testing and retrying. Under separate quotas, a heavy Codex habit was Codex’s problem. Under a shared pool, a weekend of aggressive agent work can leave a subscriber staring at a drained allowance when they open ChatGPT on Monday. The coding tool will have spent the chat budget.

Which is why the pushback has already started. Paying users are not objecting to limits existing — every subscriber already lives with quotas. They are objecting to the limits becoming shared: to one product’s appetite quietly taxing the other. Someone who bought a plan for both tools now has to budget between them, and “budget” is a word nobody enjoys meeting inside a product they already pay for. Expect the loudest complaints from exactly the users OpenAI values most — the ones who run Codex hard, every day.

The practical advice is boring but real: from December, treat the allowance like a phone battery. Know that long agent runs are the heavy drain, glance at the balance before launching a big job, and do not be surprised mid-task. Teams on Business plans get the same lesson at group scale, where one person’s marathon coding session draws on the same pool as everyone else’s afternoon of questions.

Why it matters: pricing is where AI’s promises meet its costs, and this is the clearest signal yet of where subscriptions are heading. Chat and code now compete for one budget because, underneath, they were always competing for the same machines. OpenAI has simply decided that from December, you will be able to see the meter — and feel it.

Policy · The WebNov. 2

Two Lines of robots.txt Now Block All Four AI Giants From Training on Your Site

The most consequential update to the web’s oldest protocol fits in two lines:
User-Agent: __usage_ai-training
Disallow: /
On October 30, 2026, Google, OpenAI, Microsoft and Anthropic jointly backed a single new robots.txt policy token — __usage_ai-training — that lets any publisher block AI training across all four platforms at once. Until now, opting out meant blocking each crawler separately: GPTBot for OpenAI, Google-Extended for Google, ClaudeBot for Anthropic, and so on down a list that never stopped growing. One token replaces the whole chore.

The signal is defined in an IETF Internet-Draft by Kevin Kelley, an engineer at Anthropic, and its scope is carefully drawn. It controls training only. Adding the token does not affect search indexing or rankings — your pages can still be found, listed and clicked exactly as before. The line it draws is between being read by a search engine, which sends you readers, and being absorbed into a model, which may not. For publishers, that distinction is the entire argument.

The rollout, though, runs on four different clocks, so read the dates before you celebrate. Anthropic has already begun implementing the signal. OpenAI will follow in the coming months. Microsoft’s support starts February 1, 2027, and Google’s arrives in 2027. A publisher who adds the two lines today is making a statement all four giants have agreed to honor — just not all starting today.

And keep the caveats in view. The token is still a draft, not a ratified standard; its force comes from the four companies’ joint backing rather than from law. It cannot undo anything — content already collected stays collected, and no robots.txt entry reaches backward in time. Meta, notably, has expressed support without committing, so the fifth giant is applauding from the doorway rather than standing at the line. If you run a site, the two lines are still worth adding now: the statement costs nothing and the fence goes up on each company’s announced date.

Why it matters: this lands in the middle of publisher lawsuits over AI training, which is exactly why it matters. The industry is trying to build a consent mechanism before courts build one for it. Two lines in a humble text file — the web’s original handshake between sites and crawlers — may turn out to be the most consequential standard of the AI era, provided the giants keep their word on schedule.

Sources: IETF Internet-Draft · Google · OpenAI · Microsoft · Anthropic · Nov. 2, 2026

OpenAI Is Ending All-You-Can-Prompt — ChatGPT and Codex Are Merging Into One Meter

OpenAI is combining ChatGPT’s Chat and Work modes into a single interface — and starting in December, Plus, Pro and Business plans will draw ChatGPT and Codex from one shared, token-based quota. Two products, one meter, and the quiet end of the flat-rate era: from now on, everything you ask costs from the same bucket.

Here is the change in plain terms. Today, your subscription buys you two loosely separate allowances: conversations in ChatGPT, and coding-agent work in Codex, each with its own limits that most people never think about. Under the new system, the walls come down. Chat and Work become one surface, and underneath it sits one pool of tokens. A long Codex session chewing through a codebase and an afternoon of casual chatting pull from the same balance. When the bucket empties, both slow down together — the way a phone plan’s data cap never cared whether you spent it on maps or movies.

Why now? Because the economics of “unlimited-feeling” broke. A chat exchange costs OpenAI fractions of a cent; an autonomous coding agent that reasons, tests and retries for an hour costs real money — and agent usage is the fastest-growing load on the system. Flat pricing worked when everyone’s usage looked alike. It stops working when one subscriber costs a hundred times more than the next. A shared token meter is OpenAI admitting, in product form, that compute is a metered utility now, like electricity — and utilities do not do all-you-can-eat.

For subscribers, the practical shift is awareness. Most Plus and Pro users will never hit the shared quota with normal chatting; the people who feel this first are the power users who run Codex agents all day and also live in ChatGPT — previously two allowances, now one appetite. Business plans face the same math at team scale, where one engineer’s weekend agent run can nibble at a pool the whole office shares attention with. Expect a new literacy to form fast: checking the meter before launching a big job, the way you check your battery before a long drive.

Preparing is simpler than it sounds. Learn roughly what things cost: short chats are cheap, long documents and marathon agent sessions are not. Save the heavy agent runs for when they matter, keep exploratory prompting in lighter modes, and watch your first December bill-cycle the way you watched your first roaming charges — once, carefully, and then it becomes habit. Rivals will market themselves against the meter for a while, but do not mistake this for one company’s greed: every lab selling agents under a flat price is doing the same math tonight.

Why it matters: subscriptions sold the feeling of infinity; agents ended it. By merging ChatGPT and Codex into one token meter, OpenAI is setting the template the whole industry will copy — AI priced like the utility it has become, with a gauge you will learn to read.

Google Just Got a Haiku From Orbit — AI Is Now Running on a Satellite

Somewhere above the Arctic, a Google AI model just wrote its first poem. Engineers uplinked a prompt to Gemma 4, Google’s open model, running on TPU chips aboard a Project Suncatcher prototype satellite — and during a ground-station pass over Svalbard, Norway, the satellite sent back a haiku. It is a small artifact and an enormous sentence to be able to say truthfully: an AI model has now done inference in orbit and phoned the result home.

Keep both feet on the ground about the hardware, though, because the specs are charmingly humble. The prototype carries four TPUs and its solar setup generates up to about 1 kilowatt — roughly a kettle’s worth of power. That is enough to run exactly one chip for about 20 minutes at a time. No training runs, no serving traffic, no data center in the sky: a single accelerator, sipping sunlight, answering one prompt during the few minutes the satellite is within reach of a polar ground station, then going quiet until the next pass.

So why bother? Because the two walls Earth-based AI keeps hitting are land and power. Data centers need acres, grid connections and cooling, and every region on Earth is starting to argue about all three. Orbit offers the one resource that is genuinely abundant up there: unfiltered sunlight, nearly around the clock. Project Suncatcher is Google’s long bet that computing eventually follows energy — the same logic that put server farms next to hydroelectric dams, pointed at the biggest power source available. A haiku is how you prove the plumbing works before you build the city.

That is the right frame for this milestone: a hello-world, not a product. Orbital computing still has to survive radiation, thermal swings, launch costs and the small problem that you cannot send a technician up to reboot anything. But every infrastructure era starts with a stunt that looks like a toy — the first email, the first web page, the first photo from a phone. A poem from a satellite with one working chip is this era’s version, and it arrived ahead of most people’s schedules.

Why it matters: AI’s growth is now limited by electricity and land, not ideas. Google just demonstrated the first working proof that inference can happen in orbit — twenty minutes and one haiku at a time. The orbital data center is not here, but it is no longer a slide in a pitch deck either.
